### 82. ✅ FIXED · [P3] Manual order schema accepts unvalidated/negative numeric strings for qty, notional, and prices

- **Lane:** security-auth-tenant · **Category:** trading · **Verifier:** confirmed · **Finder confidence:** medium
- **Location:** `src/lib/validators.ts:303-338`

**Description.** placeBrokerOrderSchema (used by POST /api/broker/orders) types qty, notional, limitPrice, stopPrice, takeProfitPrice, stopLossPrice as `z.string().min(1)` / `z.string().optional()` with NO numeric parsing, sign check, or NaN/Infinity guard. The route forwards these raw strings straight to client.placeOrder() (broker/orders/route.ts:159-171). A caller can submit qty='-5', qty='1e9', notional='0', limitPrice='-10', or a non-numeric string and the request passes Zod validation; correctness then depends entirely on the downstream broker rejecting it. The project's own rule is to guard external/computed numbers with !(x > 0) before money math rather than relying on a third party.

**Impact.** Malformed or negative order quantities/prices reach the broker placement call unvalidated. Best case the broker rejects (wasted call + confusing error); worst case a permissive/fractional path or future non-Alpaca broker mis-sizes or mis-prices a real order. No server-side floor on order size.

**Suggested fix.** Validate each numeric field with a refinement that parses to a finite number and enforces sign/range, e.g. qty/notional must parse to a finite value > 0, prices must parse to finite > 0; reject NaN/Infinity/negative. Use a shared numeric-string validator so every order field is guarded consistently.

**Verifier verdict:** `confirmed` — corrected severity P3.

> The schema-level claim is fully accurate. placeBrokerOrderSchema (validators.ts:303-347) types qty/notional as z.string().min(1).optional() and limitPrice/stopPrice/takeProfitPrice/stopLossPrice as z.string().optional() — no parseFloat, no sign/range check, no NaN/Infinity guard. The cross-field refinements only enforce qty-XOR-notional and notional-must-be-market/day-ioc; none constrain the numeric value. The route (broker/orders/route.ts:159-171) forwards parsed.data.{qty,notional,limitPrice,stopPrice,takeProfitPrice,stopLossPrice} raw into client.placeOrder(), and the Alpaca client (brokers.ts:387-421) copies them straight into the outbound JSON payload (payload.qty = params.qty, payload.notional, payload.limit_price, take_profit.limit_price, stop_loss.stop_price) with zero numeric coercion before POSTing. So qty='-5', qty='1e9', notional='0', limitPrice='-10', or a non-numeric string all pass server validation and reach the broker — confirmed by the test file, which only ever feeds well-formed positive strings ('10','5','300.50') and asserts nothing about negative/zero/NaN rejection. This is the only server-side gate on order size/price; the > 0 floor that exists (trade/[symbol]/page.tsx validate(), lines 179-208) is purely client-side and bypassable by curl, exactly the defense-in-depth gap the project's own '!(x > 0) before money math' rule targets. Impact is real but bounded: the endpoint is auth + CSRF + tier('trader') gated and acts only on the caller's OWN broker connection (no cross-tenant/IDOR/privilege dimension — the bad actor is the account owner). With the current primary broker (Alpaca) a malformed order is rejected (422 → caught → ORDER_REJECTED audit → sanitized 400), so today's realistic outcome is a wasted round-trip + confusing error, not money loss; the 'mis-sizes/mis-prices a real order' worst case is hedged on a future/permissive broker (and indeed IBKR/Tradier paths run toNumber() that would silently coerce bad input). Genuine validation/defense-in-depth gap, but the broker-rejects mitigation plus self-only blast radius make P2 slightly inflated — P3 fits.

**Evidence:** src/lib/validators.ts:303-347 (qty/notional = z.string().min(1).optional(); limitPrice/stopPrice/takeProfitPrice/stopLossPrice = z.string().optional(); refinements only check XOR + market/TIF/bracket constraints, no numeric guard); src/app/api/broker/orders/route.ts:119-125 (safeParse), 159-171 (raw strings forwarded to client.placeOrder); src/lib/brokers.ts:397-420 (Alpaca: payload.notional/qty/limit_price/stop_price and bracket take_profit/stop_loss assigned raw, then JSON.stringify POST at 423-430, no parse/sign/finite check); src/lib/brokers.ts:828,832-833,1262 (IBKR/Tradier toNumber() on qty/prices — silent coercion of bad input); src/app/dashboard/trade/[symbol]/page.tsx:184-206 (client-only > 0 validation, bypassable); tests/unit/validators.test.ts:190-223 (only positive well-formed inputs tested; no negative/zero/NaN rejection asserted)

### 83. ✅ FIXED · [P3] Checkout success_url/cancel_url accept arbitrary external URLs (open-redirect to attacker domain after Stripe checkout)

- **Lane:** security-secrets-routes · **Category:** security · **Verifier:** uncertain · **Finder confidence:** medium
- **Location:** `src/app/api/billing/checkout/route.ts:32-38, 128-149`

**Description.** checkoutSchema validates successUrl/cancelUrl only as z.string().url() (line 35-37), and they are passed straight into stripe.checkout.sessions.create as success_url/cancel_url (line 148-149) with no same-origin/allow-list check. Because the route is auth+CSRF protected the immediate victim is the authenticated user themselves, but a crafted link or an XSS/CSRF-adjacent flow that triggers checkout with a chosen success_url lands the user on an attacker-controlled page immediately after a legitimate Stripe payment flow, where {CHECKOUT_SESSION_ID} is also handed to the attacker domain. This is a classic open-redirect on a post-payment surface and weakens trust in the billing flow.

**Impact.** Post-checkout open redirect to an arbitrary domain (with the Stripe session id), usable for phishing on a high-trust payment surface.

**Suggested fix.** Restrict successUrl/cancelUrl to the app's own origin: parse the URL and reject any host not equal to NEXT_PUBLIC_BASE_URL's host (or accept only path strings and prepend baseUrl server-side). Default-only is safest unless a genuine multi-origin need exists.

**Verifier verdict:** `uncertain` — corrected severity P3.

> The code is exactly as described: checkoutSchema validates successUrl/cancelUrl only as z.string().url() (route.ts:35,37) and passes them unfiltered into stripe.checkout.sessions.create as success_url/cancel_url (route.ts:148-149, 129-130) with no same-origin/allow-list check. So the static observation is real and the proposed same-origin hardening is legitimate. However, the claimed impact ("classic open-redirect," "phishing on a high-trust payment surface") is materially overstated. (1) The route is auth + CSRF protected: requireAuthWithCsrf runs requireCsrf() then getSession() (auth.ts:157-163), and the route is NOT on the CSRF-exempt list (CLAUDE.md exempts only auth/login, auth/register, auth/logout, csrf, cron/*), so an attacker cannot forge this POST cross-site. (2) There is no reflected/unauthenticated path — unlike a real open-redirect there is no link an attacker can hand a victim; setting a malicious successUrl requires a CSRF-token-bearing authenticated POST, i.e. an existing XSS foothold (which would dwarf this issue) or the user attacking themselves. (3) Neither legitimate call site ever sends these fields — upgrade-button.tsx:116 and dashboard/billing/page.tsx:151 both send only { priceId }, so the override fields are unused flexibility hooks. (4) The finding itself concedes the victim is the authenticated user, and the leaked {CHECKOUT_SESSION_ID} is the user's own session id. This is defense-in-depth hardening, not an exploitable open-redirect. Marked uncertain rather than clean false_positive because the missing input restriction genuinely exists and is worth fixing, but the impact as written is a non-issue under real call conditions.

**Evidence:** src/app/api/billing/checkout/route.ts:35,37 (z.string().url().optional() — no origin check); route.ts:128-130 (baseUrl default vs client override); route.ts:148-149 (success_url/cancel_url passed straight to Stripe); route.ts:41 (requireAuthWithCsrf gate); src/lib/auth.ts:157-163 (requireCsrf + getSession, no exemption); src/components/tiers/upgrade-button.tsx:113-117 and src/app/dashboard/billing/page.tsx:148-152 (both legitimate callers send only { priceId }, never successUrl/cancelUrl); CLAUDE.md CSRF-exempt list does not include billing/checkout.

### 84. ✅ FIXED · [P3] Concurrent duplicate webhook deliveries can both pass the pre-check and double-execute append-only handlers (duplicate audit/refund rows)

- **Lane:** security-secrets-routes · **Category:** concurrency · **Verifier:** confirmed · **Finder confidence:** high
- **Location:** `src/app/api/webhooks/stripe/route.ts:107-140`

**Description.** Idempotency is enforced by a SELECT pre-check (line 107-115) followed by handleEvent() then an INSERT ... onConflictDoNothing after success (line 132-140). Between the SELECT and the INSERT there is an await-heavy window (handleEvent calls stripe.subscriptions.retrieve + DB writes). Stripe's at-least-once semantics can deliver the same event ID twice nearly simultaneously; both requests see no row in the pre-check and both run handleEvent. The code comment (line 95-103) argues tier writes are set-to-value and therefore safe, which is true for applyTierFromSubscription, but handleChargeRefunded (line 408-421) and handleInvoicePaymentFailed (line 367-378) only APPEND audit rows — a concurrent double-delivery writes duplicate BILLING_REFUNDED / BILLING_PAYMENT_FAILED audit entries (and duplicate notification side effects elsewhere). The onConflictDoNothing dedup row is only written AFTER the handler, so it cannot prevent concurrent double-execution, only sequential redelivery.

**Impact.** Rare duplicate audit-trail rows and duplicate user-facing billing side effects on concurrent redelivery; pollutes the hash-chained audit/billing trail rather than causing money loss.

**Suggested fix.** Insert the dedup row inside the same transaction as the handler's side effects (relying on the unique constraint to reject the second concurrent run), or wrap handleEvent + dedup-insert in a transaction with the dedup INSERT first using onConflictDoNothing and short-circuiting when zero rows are affected. For the append-only handlers, make the audit/notification writes idempotent on event.id.

**Verifier verdict:** `confirmed` — corrected severity P3.

> The code matches the finding exactly. Idempotency is a SELECT pre-check (route.ts:107-115) → handleEvent() → onConflictDoNothing INSERT (route.ts:132-140). Between the SELECT and the dedup INSERT there is a genuine await-heavy window: for charge.refunded, findUserByStripeCustomer (DB read) + writeAudit (a full DB transaction); for invoice.payment_failed, DB read + DB update + writeAudit. Two near-simultaneous deliveries of the same evt_xxx would both find no row at lines 107-112 and both execute handleEvent. The author's own comment (route.ts:95-103) concedes "a rare *concurrent* duplicate delivery that slips past the pre-check below is harmless" on the grounds that handlers are "state-idempotent (tier + billing status are set-to-value)." That justification holds for applyTierFromSubscription, handleSubscriptionDeleted, and the billingStatus='past_due' UPDATE in handleInvoicePaymentFailed — all set-to-value. It does NOT cover the append-only writeAudit calls in handleChargeRefunded (route.ts:408-421, BILLING_REFUNDED) and handleInvoicePaymentFailed (route.ts:367-378, BILLING_PAYMENT_FAILED). writeAudit (audit.ts:126-186) serializes via pg_advisory_xact_lock but unconditionally INSERTs with no dedup on event.id (the audit resourceId for a refund is charge.id, and there is no unique constraint regardless). So a concurrent double-execution does append two valid audit rows. The finding correctly identifies the gap between the comment's reasoning and the append-only handlers. Impact is real but minor, and the finding's own framing matches: the duplicate rows are independently valid and correctly hash-chained (advisory lock serializes them), so verifyAuditChain still passes — the chain is NOT corrupted, just carries a redundant entry. No money loss (refunds are issued by Stripe, not by this handler, which only records), and no duplicate user-facing email (handleInvoicePaymentFailed delegates outreach to Stripe's built-in customer emails per route.ts:380-382). The "duplicate notification side effects elsewhere" clause is speculative/unverified but the finding hedges it. The window also requires truly simultaneous same-event delivery, which is uncommon. P3 (the finding's own severity) is accurate and not inflated; "pollutes the hash-chained trail" slightly overstates it (duplicate valid row, not corruption), but the core concurrency claim is sound.

**Evidence:** src/app/api/webhooks/stripe/route.ts:107-115 (SELECT pre-check), :119 (await handleEvent), :132-140 (post-handler onConflictDoNothing INSERT), :95-103 (author comment justifying concurrent dup as "harmless" via set-to-value state-idempotency), :367-378 (handleInvoicePaymentFailed append-only BILLING_PAYMENT_FAILED audit), :380-382 (delegates user email to Stripe, so no duplicate notification from our code), :408-421 (handleChargeRefunded append-only BILLING_REFUNDED audit; only logs, no money movement); src/lib/audit.ts:126-186 (writeAudit unconditionally INSERTs, no event.id dedup; advisory lock only serializes, both rows still valid and chain-intact); src/lib/db/schema/stripe.ts:25-26 (eventId is the only dedup key, in stripe_events_processed, written after handler)

### 85. ✅ FIXED · [P3] ENCRYPTION_KEY env validation checks character length (32+), not byte length, so a 32-char hex key (16 bytes) passes boot validation then crashes all crypto

- **Lane:** security-secrets-routes · **Category:** reliability · **Verifier:** confirmed · **Finder confidence:** high
- **Location:** `src/lib/env.ts:22-24`

**Description.** env.ts validates ENCRYPTION_KEY as z.string().min(32) in production (line 22-24), i.e. at least 32 characters. But getEncryptionKey() in src/lib/crypto.ts:16-27 requires the decoded key to be exactly 32 BYTES — 64 hex chars OR 44 base64 chars. A 32-character hex string decodes to only 16 bytes and a 32-character base64 string decodes to 24 bytes; both pass env validation but cause getEncryptionKey() to throw CryptoError('ENCRYPTION_KEY must be 32 bytes...') on the first encrypt/decrypt. validateEnv() therefore gives false confidence: the boot 'fail fast' check passes while every system_config decrypt, broker-token decrypt, and Stripe-secret read throws at runtime.

**Impact.** A subtly-too-short encryption key passes the documented fail-fast boot check yet breaks all at-rest crypto at first use (config reads, broker tokens), producing confusing runtime failures instead of a clear boot error.

**Suggested fix.** Validate ENCRYPTION_KEY by decoding it and asserting 32 bytes (mirror getEncryptionKey: try hex→32 bytes, else base64→32 bytes) in env.ts via a .refine(), so a wrong-length key fails loudly at boot rather than at first crypto call.

**Verifier verdict:** `confirmed` — corrected severity P3.

> The finding is accurate on every point. env.ts:22-24 validates ENCRYPTION_KEY in production with z.string().min(32), which counts CHARACTERS, not decoded bytes. crypto.ts:16-27 getEncryptionKey() requires the DECODED buffer to be exactly 32 bytes (buf.length === 32), accepting hex (64 chars) or base64 (44 chars). I verified the byte math directly: a 32-char hex string decodes to 16 bytes, a 32-char base64 string decodes to 24 bytes, and only 64 hex chars yields 32 bytes. So a 32-char key in either encoding passes env validation but throws CryptoError('ENCRYPTION_KEY must be 32 bytes...') the first time getEncryptionKey() runs. That function is the sole gateway for both encrypt() (crypto.ts:42) and decrypt() (crypto.ts:80), which back system-config API-key reads and broker-token decrypts. The 'fail fast at boot' contract the finding relies on is real: instrumentation.ts:7-16 calls validateEnv() and process.exit(1) in production on failure, and the env.ts header comment (lines 4-6) and instrumentation comment (lines 4-6) both state the check exists precisely to avoid 'undecipherable broker tokens later' — exactly the failure this gap re-introduces. So validateEnv() does give false confidence for a 32-char-but-not-32-byte key. The impact is correctly characterized but inherently limited in likelihood: a correctly generated key (openssl rand -hex 32 = 64 chars, or rand -base64 32 = 44 chars) passes both checks; only a hand-crafted exactly-32-char key in the wrong encoding triggers it. The failure mode is a loud throw (not a silent weak-key use or any security/tenant-isolation bypass), so the consequence is a confusing deferred crash rather than data loss. That matches P3 — a real reliability/DX footgun, neither inflated nor a false positive. The proposed fix (.refine() decoding to 32 bytes, mirroring getEncryptionKey) is appropriate.

**Evidence:** src/lib/env.ts:22-24 (ENCRYPTION_KEY: z.string().min(32) — character count); src/lib/crypto.ts:16-27 (getEncryptionKey requires buf.length === 32 bytes, hex or base64, else throws CryptoError); src/lib/crypto.ts:42,80 (encrypt/decrypt both call getEncryptionKey); src/instrumentation.ts:7-16 (validateEnv() at boot, process.exit(1) in prod — the 'fail fast' contract); byte math verified: 32-char hex -> 16 bytes, 32-char base64 -> 24 bytes, 64-char hex -> 32 bytes

---

### 86. ✅ FIXED · [P3] decrypt failure in getConfig silently falls back to a possibly-stale env secret, which can permanently drop Stripe webhook (tier-grant) deliveries

- **Lane:** security-secrets-routes · **Category:** reliability · **Verifier:** confirmed · **Finder confidence:** medium
- **Location:** `src/lib/system-config.ts:116-141`

**Description.** getConfig() decrypts the DB row, and on ANY decrypt failure (line 119-126: tampered ciphertext OR wrong/rotated ENCRYPTION_KEY) it logs and falls through to process.env[key] (line 136-138), then caches that fallback for 60s. For STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET this means: if the encrypted DB value can no longer be decrypted (e.g. ENCRYPTION_KEY was rotated without re-encrypting the row, or partial corruption), the webhook route (src/app/api/webhooks/stripe/route.ts:70-84) verifies every incoming Stripe signature against the OLD env-fallback secret. Stripe signs with the current per-endpoint secret, so constructEvent() throws, the route returns 400 INVALID_SIGNATURE (line 89-92), and Stripe stops retrying after its window — the tier grant / downgrade event is lost permanently with no alert. The fallback masks a hard configuration error as a per-event signature failure. Same silent-substitution risk applies to STRIPE_SECRET_KEY and the LLM keys, but billing has the worst blast radius (paid users never get upgraded; canceled users never get downgraded).

**Impact.** On ENCRYPTION_KEY rotation or DB-row corruption, all Stripe billing webhooks silently fail signature verification against a stale env secret; users pay but never receive their tier, and cancellations never downgrade — revenue/entitlement drift with no loud failure.

**Suggested fix.** Distinguish 'no row' from 'row present but undecryptable'. When a row exists but decrypt() throws, do NOT fall back to env for security-critical secrets — surface a loud error/metric (the secret is misconfigured, fail closed) rather than silently using a different value. At minimum, emit a high-severity alert/metric on decrypt-failure-then-env-fallback so a rotated key surfaces immediately instead of as mysterious 400s on every Stripe delivery.

**Verifier verdict:** `confirmed` — corrected severity P3.

> The code behaves exactly as the finding describes. getConfig() (src/lib/system-config.ts:117-127) catches any decrypt() failure, logs log.error, and falls through with value=null; lines 136-138 then substitute process.env[key], cached 60s (line 140). The Stripe webhook route reads the secret via getStripeWebhookSecret()->getConfig("STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET") (route.ts:70) and passes it to constructEvent() (route.ts:84); on a mismatched secret constructEvent throws, caught at line 85, returning 400 INVALID_SIGNATURE with retryable:false (lines 89-92). The line-88 comment confirms the 400 is deliberately chosen so Stripe will NOT retry, so a wrong secret loses that event permanently. crypto.ts confirms decrypt() throws (not returns input) on wrong key / tampered ciphertext (lines 63-94, getEncryptionKey 16-27). So the silent-substitution mechanism and lost-billing-event consequence are genuine, and there is no metric/alert distinguishing decrypt-failure from no-row. However the impact is gated behind a narrow, operationally-unsupported misconfiguration: (1) ENCRYPTION_KEY rotation is NOT part of the supported tooling — scripts/rotate-secrets.sh only seds JWT_SECRET + CRON_SECRET (lines 177-178) and the runbook lists ENCRYPTION_KEY as a required-but-untouched var (rotate-secrets.md:190), so a rotated/corrupted ENCRYPTION_KEY is a manual operator error, not a documented flow; (2) the harmful 400 only occurs if process.env[STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET] is present AND holds a different/stale value — if env is absent the route correctly returns a retryable 503 (route.ts:71-77, Stripe retries), and if env equals the real secret there is no mismatch; (3) a corrupted ENCRYPTION_KEY would also break getStripeSecretKey() decryption (getStripeClient, stripe.ts:39-49) and every other system_config secret, so the misconfig would surface loudly across many paths rather than only as mysterious Stripe 400s. The fail-loud/observability gap is real and worth fixing, but the precondition chain (unsupported manual key rotation + stale-but-different env value) and the broad collateral failure that would expose it make P2 overstated; P3 (reliability/observability hardening) is the appropriate severity.

**Evidence:** src/lib/system-config.ts:117-127 (decrypt try/catch logs and falls through, value stays null), :136-138 (process.env[key] fallback), :140 (60s cache), :463-465 (getStripeWebhookSecret->getConfig); src/app/api/webhooks/stripe/route.ts:70 (reads secret), :84 (constructEvent), :85-92 (catch returns 400 INVALID_SIGNATURE retryable:false), :88 (comment: 400 so Stripe doesn't retry), :71-77 (absent-secret path returns 503 retryable:true — handled correctly); src/lib/crypto.ts:63-94 (decrypt throws on wrong key/tamper); src/lib/stripe.ts:39-49 (getStripeSecretKey also via getConfig, same fallback); scripts/rotate-secrets.sh:177-178 (rotation seds only JWT_SECRET/CRON_SECRET); docs/runbooks/rotate-secrets.md:190 (ENCRYPTION_KEY listed as required, untouched by tooling)
